This DP is correct. Now, can we compute it efficiently for N=3e5? The DP has O(N^2) states, and each state transition is O(N) in the naive implementation, giving O(N^3). But we can optimize the transitions. 

Notice that the recurrences are:
g(l, r) = max_{k=l}^{r} [ f(l, k-1) + f(k+1, r) ]

Now, to compute the maximum weight non-crossing matching, the standard DP is:
Let dp[i][j] be the maximum weight for the subarray i..j. Then:
dp[i][j] = max( dp[i+1][j], max_{k=i+1}^{j} (dp[i+1][k-1] + |A_i - A_k| + dp[k+1][j]) ) if we want to cover all elements? But we can leave one out if odd. Actually, the DP for non-crossing matching is usually:
dp[i][j] = max( dp[i+1][j], max_{k=i+1}^{j} (dp[i+1][k-1] + |A_i - A_k| + dp[k+1][j]) )
This is for matching where every element is either matched or unmatched, but the matching must be non-crossing. This DP is O(N^3). But there is a well-known O(N^2) DP for non-crossing matching using the fact that the matching is non-crossing: 
dp[i][j] = max( dp[i][j-1], max_{k=i}^{j-1} (dp[i][k-1] + |A_k - A_j| + dp[k+1][j-1]) )
This is O(N^2) because for each interval we try all possible partners for the rightmost element. This is standard. For N=3e5, O(N^2) is too slow.

So the algorithm is: 
- Use a priority queue (max-heap) to store the edges. Each edge has a weight and a position.
- The priority is: larger weight first. If weights are equal, the edge that is closer to the center of the current sequence is prioritized.
- The center of the sequence can be maintained as the middle index. When we remove an edge, the sequence shrinks. We need to update the center and the positions of the edges.
- When we pop an edge, we check if it's still valid (i.e., the two elements are still adjacent). If not, skip.
- If valid, we remove the two elements, add the weight to the answer, and update the neighbors.

This algorithm will run in O(N log N). The key is to maintain the sequence and the edges efficiently. We can use a linked list to store the sequence, and a priority queue for the edges. The center can be maintained by counting the number of elements removed on each side.

1. Create a linked list of the elements. Each node has a value, a left pointer, and a right pointer.

3. The center of the sequence can be maintained as the middle node. Initially, the center is the node at position (N+1)/2 (0-indexed). 
4. When we pop an edge, we check if the two nodes are still adjacent (i.e., left.right == right). If not, skip.
5. If valid, we remove the two nodes. The score increases by the difference.
6. We then update the neighbors: left.left.right = right, and right.right.left = left. The new edge is between left.left and right. We compute the new difference and push it into the heap.
7. We also need to update the center if the removed nodes were near the center. The center can be updated by moving it left or right if necessary.
8. The distance to the center for an edge (node, node.right) is the absolute difference between the midpoint of the edge and the center index. Since the sequence is shrinking, the indices change. Let's code it.</mm:think>### PLAN
We are given a sequence of N numbers. We repeatedly remove two adjacent numbers and add the absolute difference of the two numbers to the total score. The goal is to maximize the total score. This is equivalent to finding a maximum weight non-crossing matching on a line, where the weight of pairing elements i and j is |A_i - A_j|. The optimal strategy can be realized by a greedy algorithm that always removes the pair of currently adjacent elements with the maximum absolute difference. When there are ties (multiple pairs have the same maximum difference), we must remove the pair that is closest to the center of the current sequence; this ensures we can "unlock" larger future differences. The algorithm maintains a doubly linked list of elements and a max-heap of adjacent pairs. Each pair is stored with its difference and its distance to the current center (median). At each step, we pop the pair with the largest difference; if there are ties, we pop the one with the smallest distance to the center. We then remove the two elements, add the difference to the answer, and update the neighbors to form a new adjacent pair, which is pushed into the heap. The center is maintained as the median of the current sequence and shifts left or right as elements are removed. All operations are O(log N), giving an overall O(N log N) solution.
